# Bibliography

This is a historical source ledger, not a short decision bibliography. Current decisions should use a compact evidence kernel and treat the remaining entries as a watchlist. Each source earns its place by shaping design judgment
rather than merely confirming that AI in education exists.

## B01

**UNESCO (2023, updated 2026). _Guidance for generative AI in education and research_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/guidance-generative-ai-education-and-research

Why it matters: establishes a global, human-centred policy framing for GenAI in education and
research.

## B02

**UNESCO (2021, updated 2025). _AI and education: guidance for policy-makers_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/ai-and-education-guidance-policy-makers

Why it matters: gives a public-policy frame for balancing opportunity, risk, equity, and system
design.

## B03

**UNESCO (2024, updated 2026). _AI competency framework for teachers_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/ai-competency-framework-teachers

Why it matters: defines teacher competencies across human-centred mindset, ethics, AI foundations,
AI pedagogy, and professional learning.

## B04

**UNESCO (2024, updated 2026). _AI competency framework for students_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/ai-competency-framework-students

Why it matters: supports the claim that AI literacy should become part of formal curriculum rather
than remain extracurricular.

## B05

**RAND (2024). _Using Artificial Intelligence Tools in K–12 Classrooms_.**

URL: https://www.rand.org/pubs/research_reports/RRA956-21.html

Why it matters: gives early evidence on how teachers were actually using AI and how uneven training
support was.

## B06

**RAND (2025). _AI Use in Schools Is Quickly Increasing but Guidance Lags Behind_.**

URL: https://www.rand.org/pubs/research_reports/RRA4180-1.html

Why it matters: shows rapid uptake by students and teachers, paired with policy/training lag and
concern about critical thinking and false cheating accusations.

## B07

**U.S. Department of Education / ERIC (2024). _Empowering Education Leaders: A Toolkit for Safe,
Ethical, and Equitable AI Integration_.**

URL: https://eric.ed.gov/?id=ED661924

Why it matters: useful operational checklist for privacy, data security, civil rights, digital
equity, transparency, and opt-out in school contexts.

## B08

**U.S. Department of Education (2025). _Guidance on Artificial Intelligence Use in Schools_.**

URL: https://www.ed.gov/about/news/press-release/us-department-of-education-issues-guidance-artificial-intelligence-use-schools-proposes-additional-supplemental-priority

Why it matters: clarifies that responsible AI can support instructional materials, tutoring,
advising, and navigation under existing federal programs.

## B09

**OECD (2024). _The potential impact of Artificial Intelligence on equity and inclusion in
education_.**

URL: https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/the-potential-impact-of-artificial-intelligence-on-equity-and-inclusion-in-education_15df715b-en.html

Why it matters: keeps equity, privacy, cultural responsiveness, disability inclusion, and teacher
training in the centre of adoption decisions.

## B10

**OECD (2025). _AI adoption in the education system_.**

URL: https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/ai-adoption-in-the-education-system_69bd0a4a-en.html

Why it matters: frames AI adoption as a policy problem tied to disparities, curriculum, mitigation,
and system-level roadmaps.

## B11

**OECD (2026). _OECD Digital Education Outlook 2026: Exploring Effective Uses of Generative AI in
Education_** and companion blog _How to effectively use Generative AI in education_.

URLs:
- https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-digital-education-outlook-2026_062a7394-en.html
- https://www.oecd.org/en/blogs/2026/01/how-to-effectively-use-generative-ai-in-education.html

Why they matter: sharp statement that successful task performance with GenAI does not automatically
produce learning, and that purpose-built educational tools show more promise than unguided
general-purpose use.

## B12

**IES (2025). _Using Generative Artificial Intelligence to Augment Teaching and Learning in
Classrooms_** and **AmplifyGAIN**.

URLs:
- https://ies.ed.gov/funding/research/programs/education-research-and-development-centers/using-generative-artificial-intelligence-augment-teaching-and-learning-classrooms
- https://ies.ed.gov/use-work/awards/amplifygain-generative-ai-transformative-learning

Why they matter: indicate where U.S. public R&D is placing serious bets—teacher assistants, reading
support, STEM learning agents, and evidence-aligned design.

## B13

**SCALE / Stanford NSSA (2025). _Tutor CoPilot: A Human-AI Approach for Scaling Real-Time
Expertise_.**

URL: https://scale.stanford.edu/publications/tutor-copilot-human-ai-approach-scaling-real-time-expertise

Why it matters: human-AI tutoring assistance improved student topic mastery, especially for
lower-rated tutors, suggesting teacher/tutor augmentation may be a high-leverage route.

## B14

**OpenAI (2025). _Introducing study mode_.**

URL: https://openai.com/index/chatgpt-study-mode/

Why it matters: market signal that leading providers are moving toward guided-learning interactions
rather than pure answer delivery.

## B15

**Anthropic (2025). _Introducing Claude for Education_.**

URL: https://www.anthropic.com/news/introducing-claude-for-education

Why it matters: another provider signal toward reasoning-guided, campus-integrated AI rather than
generic chat alone.

## B16

**OpenAI (2025). _Working with 400,000 teachers to shape the future of AI in schools_** and **A free
version of ChatGPT built for teachers**.

URLs:
- https://openai.com/global-affairs/aft/
- https://openai.com/index/chatgpt-for-teachers/

Why they matter: signals that large-scale teacher professional learning and teacher-specific
workspaces are becoming part of the institutional adoption landscape.

## B17

**RAND (2026). _More Students Use AI for Homework, and More Believe It Harms Critical Thinking_.**

URL: https://www.rand.org/pubs/research_reports/RRA4742-1.html

Why it matters: updates the demand side of the problem—student homework use is still rising, but so
is student concern that routine AI use can erode critical thinking.

## B18

**OECD (2026). _PISA 2029 Media and Artificial Intelligence Literacy (MAIL)_.**

URL: https://www.oecd.org/en/about/projects/pisa-2029-media-and-artificial-intelligence-literacy.html

Why it matters: shows that AI literacy is becoming a formal cross-national assessment concern rather
than an optional enrichment topic.

## B19

**OECD (2026). _AI to Support Neurodivergent Learners in Vocational Education and Training_.**

URL: https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/ai-to-support-neurodivergent-learners-in-vocational-education-and-training_718d7522-en.html

Why it matters: adds a concrete inclusion lens, especially for accessibility, affordability,
teacher-capacity design, and practical guidance on how learners can use generative AI and
writing-assistance tools without breaching plagiarism rules.

## B20

**UNESCO (2025). _What you need to know about AI and the right to education_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/what-you-need-know-about-ai-and-right-education

Why it matters: sharpens the archive's rights-based layer by tying AI in education to availability,
accessibility, acceptability, adaptation, privacy, and the best interests of the child.

## B21

**OECD (2025). _AILit Framework_ public consultation.**

URL: https://www.oecd.org/en/events/public-consultations/2025/09/ailit-framework.html

Why it matters: useful implementation signal because it names the real barriers after abstract
agreement—lack of a shared understanding of AI literacy and uncertainty about how it fits into
subject teaching.


## B22

**European Commission / OECD / AILit Framework (2025-2026). _Empowering learners for the age of AI:
launch of the draft AI literacy framework and stakeholder consultations_** and **_New AI Literacy
Framework to Equip Youth in an Age of AI_**.

URLs:
- https://education.ec.europa.eu/event/empowering-learners-for-the-age-of-ai-launch-of-the-draft-ai-literacy-framework-and-stakeholder-consultations
- https://ailiteracyframework.org/blog/new-ai-literacy-framework-to-equip-youth-in-an-age-of-ai/

Why they matter: make the archive's subject-embedded turn more concrete by naming AI literacy as
cross-subject work and giving simple examples in mathematics, history/social science, and computer
science.

## B23

**UNESCO (2025, updated 2026). _Artificial intelligence in education: UNESCO advances key
competencies for teachers and learners_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/artificial-intelligence-education-unesco-advances-key-competencies-teachers-and-learners

Why it matters: provides a concrete classroom implementation signal, including an English-class
example of AI used as feedback/tutoring support rather than as a replacement author.

## B24

**European Commission (2026). _Guidelines on the ethical use of artificial intelligence and data in
teaching and learning_.**

URL: https://education.ec.europa.eu/focus-topics/digital-education/action-plan/ethical-guidelines-for-educators-on-using-ai

Why it matters: provides a current practical governance signal for schools, tying rising AI use to
the need for context-based ethical decisions, AI Act compliance, and critical AI literacy rather
than ad hoc classroom improvisation.

## B25

**UNESCO IESALC (2025). _Artificial intelligence in higher education: A strategic agenda for
institutional transformation_.**

URL: https://www.iesalc.unesco.org/en/articles/artificial-intelligence-higher-education-strategic-agenda-institutional-transformation

Why it matters: adds a higher-education governance signal that institutions need clear disclosure
protocols, human oversight, faculty capacity, secure infrastructure, and institutional governance.

## B26

**UNESCO IdeasLAB (2025). _What's worth measuring? The future of assessment in the AI age_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/whats-worth-measuring-future-assessment-ai-age

Why it matters: although it is a think piece rather than formal guidance, it is a sharp public
synthesis of why detector-led assessment is unstable and why process evidence, dialogue, and
contextualized tasks are becoming more important.


## B27

**U.S. Department of Justice / ADA.gov (2024, current compliance guidance 2025-2026). _Fact Sheet:
New Rule on the Accessibility of Web Content and Mobile Apps Provided by State and Local
Governments_.**

URL: https://www.ada.gov/resources/2024-03-08-web-rule/

Why it matters: turns accessibility into a concrete institutional floor for public educational
web/app surfaces, including contractor- and vendor-provided content, while keeping
effective-communication and reasonable-modification duties in force.

## B28

**U.S. Department of Education, Office for Civil Rights (2024). _Avoiding the Discriminatory Use of
Artificial Intelligence_.**

URL: https://www.ed.gov/media/document/avoiding-discriminatory-use-of-artificial-intelligence-112274.pdf

Why it matters: clarifies that school and postsecondary AI use remains subject to federal
civil-rights law, which matters for procurement, deployment, support routing, and appeals.


## B29

**OECD (2025). _Trends in Adult Learning_.**

URL: https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/trends-in-adult-learning_ec0624a6-en.html

Why it matters: shows that adult-learning participation is uneven and that much training is short
and compliance-driven, which strengthens the case for more inclusive and responsive
lifelong-learning routes under AI-era labour-market change.

## B30

**UNESCO Institute for Lifelong Learning (2025). _Digital Empowerment for Adult Educators in the Age
of AI and Emerging Technologies_.**

URL: https://www.uil.unesco.org/en/articles/uil-launches-new-open-source-course-digital-empowerment-adult-educators-age-ai

Why it matters: concrete signal that adult educators in formal, non-formal, and informal settings
now need AI-related capability, and that accessible, self-paced public training is becoming part of
the response.

## B31

**California Community Colleges Chancellor's Office (2026). _Developing AI Literacy Across the
California Community Colleges_.**

URL: https://www.cccco.edu/-/media/CCCCO-Website/docs/memo/ai-literacy-memo-a11y.pdf

Why it matters: a system-level example of open-access postsecondary AI literacy that spans students,
faculty, staff, and administrators rather than treating AI education as a niche speciality.

## B32

**U.S. Department of Education, OCTAE (2026). _Modification Requirements for WIOA State Plans for
PYs 2026 and 2027_.**

URL: https://www.ed.gov/sites/ed/files/2026-01/octae-program-memo-26-1.pdf

Why it matters: makes navigable public workforce pathways, AI literacy and skills development,
AI-powered tools, and integrated career routes explicit planning concerns rather than optional
extras.

## B33

**UNESCO Institute for Lifelong Learning (2025-2026). _Urban upskilling: Cultivating future skills
in learning cities_** and **_UNESCO Learning City Award_**.

URLs:
- https://www.uil.unesco.org/en/articles/unesco-global-network-learning-cities-webinar-urban-upskilling-cultivating-future-skills-learning
- https://www.uil.unesco.org/en/learning-cities/unesco-learning-city-award

Why they matter: support the idea that cities can function as lifelong-learning infrastructure by
connecting education, training, cultural institutions, civil society, and employers around inclusive
local skill development.

## B34

**California State Library (2025-2026). _California Libraries Learn_** and **_California Library
Literacy and English Acquisition Services Legislative Report_.**

URLs:
- https://www.library.ca.gov/services/to-libraries/california-libraries-learn/
- https://www.library.ca.gov/uploads/2025/11/2025-California-Library-Literacy-Services-Legislative-Report.pdf

Why they matter: concrete signal that public libraries already operate as adult-learning
infrastructure and can plausibly serve as low-barrier AI-literacy entry points rather than being
treated as peripheral to education with AI.

## B35

**U.S. Department of Labor (2026). _Artificial Intelligence Literacy Framework_.**

URL: https://www.dol.gov/sites/dolgov/files/ETA/advisories/TEN/2025/TEN%2007-25/TEN%2007-25%20%28complete%20document%29.pdf

Why it matters: gives workforce agencies, providers, educators, and employers a common foundation
for AI literacy and explicitly places AI literacy inside WIOA services, orientations, and
career-navigation contexts.

## B36

**Jobs for the Future (2026). _AI Usage in Education is Growing, But Gaps in Guidance Persist, New
Survey Finds_.**

URL: https://www.jff.org/newsroom/press-releases/ai-usage-in-education-is-growing-but-gaps-in-guidance-persist-new-survey-finds/

Why it matters: recent signal that institutional support is increasing but policy clarity is still
uneven, strengthening the case for clearer public first-contact and referral norms instead of
informal shadow guidance.

## B37

**Public Library Association / American Library Association (2024-2026). _Digital Literacy_** and
**_Public Library Association expands digital literacy training funding opportunity, powered by
AT&T_.**

URLs:
- https://www.ala.org/pla/initiatives/digitalliteracy
- https://www.ala.org/news/2024/07/public-library-association-expands-digital-literacy-training-funding-opportunity

Why they matter: show that public libraries already provide free digital-skills courses,
individualized digital-navigator support, and measurable low-barrier learning infrastructure that
can plausibly host AI-learning first contact.

## B38

**American Library Association (2025). _Access to Digital Resources and Services: An Interpretation
of the Library Bill of Rights_.**

URL: https://www.ala.org/advocacy/intfreedom/librarybill/interpretations/digital

Why it matters: adds a rights-and-privacy floor for library-based public learning routes, including
equitable training, due process when access is limited, and skepticism toward vendor or system
restrictions that silently curtail user rights.

## B39

**California Community Colleges Chancellor's Office (2025). _Artificial Intelligence Workplan_.**

URL: https://www.cccco.edu/-/media/CCCCO-Website/docs/vision2030/artificial-intelligence-workplan.pdf

Why it matters: concrete system example of community colleges acting as bridge institutions through
regional consortia, workforce partnerships, AI literacy for all roles, and scalable local-to-system
learning routes.

## B40

**U.S. Department of Labor (2026). _TEGL No. 07-25: Modification Requirements for WIOA State Plans
for PYs 2026 and 2027_.**

URL: https://www.dol.gov/node/185193

Why it matters: explicitly encourages states to capture, verify, communicate, and make portable
skills and learning assertions, which supports the archive's learner-controlled portability rail.

## B41

**Credential Engine (2025). _Recognition of Prior Learning CTDL Terms Proposal_.**

URL: https://credentialengine.org/plrproposal/

Why it matters: makes prior-learning recognition machine-readable by modeling policies, accepted
evidence, evaluation methods, and outcomes rather than treating recognition as an invisible local
black box.

## B42

**1EdTech (current 2025-2026). _Comprehensive Learner Record Standard_.**

URL: https://www.1edtech.org/standards/clr

Why it matters: supports a learner-controlled, interoperable bundle of verifiable achievements with
evidence pointers and multi-issuer records rather than one vendor-locked transcript.

## B43

**W3C (2025). _Verifiable Credentials Data Model v2.0_.**

URL: https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-data-model-2.0/

Why it matters: provides the open issuer-holder-verifier model and privacy/security/accessibility
baseline for machine-verifiable learning claims.

## B44

**Europass / European Commission (2025-2026). _European Digital Credentials for Learning_** and
**_European Digital Credentials_**.

URLs:
- https://europass.europa.eu/en/european-digital-credentials-learning
- https://europass.europa.eu/en/european-digital-credentials

Why they matter: show a public-sector implementation path for verifiable digital learning
credentials stored in learner wallets and used in further training and recognition procedures.

## B45

**American Council on Education (current 2025-2026). _The ACE National Guide_.**

URL: https://www.acenet.edu/National-Guide/Pages/default.aspx

Why it matters: keeps the archive honest that some external learning can count toward formal credit,
but only through recognised institutional evaluation and published equivalency logic rather than
automatic transfer.

## B46

**California Community Colleges Chancellor's Office (current 2025-2026). _Credit for Prior
Learning_.**

URL: https://www.cccco.edu/About-Us/Chancellors-Office/Divisions/Educational-Services-and-Support/credit-for-prior-learning

Why it matters: gives a concrete open-access postsecondary recognition menu—credit by examination,
portfolio review, industry certification evaluation, JST evaluation, and standardized exams—rather
than treating recognition as a vague transfer promise.

## B47

**California Community Colleges Chancellor's Office (2025). _Credit for Prior Learning Funding
(2025-26)_.**

URL: https://www.cccco.edu/-/media/CCCCO-Website/docs/memo/ess-25-82-credit-for-prior-learning-funding-a11y.pdf

Why it matters: shows a large public bridge system treating CPL as infrastructure for working adults
and noncredit learners, with proactive offering, documentation, and system-building expectations
rather than one-off exceptions.

## B48

**American Council on Education (current 2025-2026). _Colleges & Universities: Granting Credit With
the National Guide_.**

URL: https://www.acenet.edu/National-Guide/Pages/Granting-Credit.aspx

Why it matters: keeps stronger academic recognition tied to faculty evaluation, explicit credit
recommendations, and recurring review rather than improvised trust in outside claims.

## B49

**American Council on Education (current 2025-2026). _Institutional CPL Best Practices_.**

URL: https://www.acenet.edu/Programs-Services/Pages/Credit-Transcripts/Institutional-CPL-Best-Practices.aspx

Why it matters: reinforces that institutions should publish and operationalize CPL processes
deliberately to support adult learner success rather than rely on hidden case-by-case discretion.

## B50

**CEDEFOP (current 2025-2026). _Validation of non-formal and informal learning_** and **_VET and
qualifications across borders_.**

URLs:
- https://www.cedefop.europa.eu/en/projects/validation-non-formal-and-informal-learning
- https://www.cedefop.europa.eu/en/themes/vet-qualifications-across-borders

Why they matter: keep the archive honest that learning outside formal classrooms can and should
count, but only through explicit identification, documentation, assessment, validation, and
cross-border/cross-sector transfer principles.

## B51

**American Council on Education (current 2025-2026). _Learning Evaluations FAQ_.**

URL: https://www.acenet.edu/Programs-Services/Pages/Credit-Transcripts/CREDIT-Evaluations-FAQ.aspx

Why it matters: adds the archive's version-control signal for standing recognition by stating that
materially changed offerings are evaluated separately and that stronger academic recognition may
still route through portfolio or other experiential-learning assessment.

## B52

**NCCRS / New York State Board of Regents (current 2025-2026). _Program Policies and Procedures_**
and **_Introduction and Program Philosophy_.**

URLs:
- https://www.nationalccrs.org/policies-procedures
- https://www.nationalccrs.org/content/introduction-and-program

Why they matter: make recurring review, dated exhibits, active periods, and advisory-but-published
credit recommendations explicit, which helps the archive specify how standing lists should inherit
review cadence rather than trust provider names indefinitely.

## B53

**City University of New York (current 2025-2026). _Guidance for College Implementation_**,
**_Credit for Prior Learning_**, and **_Transfer Explorer: Non-CUNY Courses, Trainings, and
Exams_.**

URLs:
- https://www.cuny.edu/academics/academic-policy/credit-prior-learning/cpl-materials-resources/guidance-for-college-implementation/
- https://www.cuny.edu/academics/academic-policy/credit-prior-learning/
- https://explorer.cuny.edu/noncuny

Why they matter: provide a concrete public-system example of standing CPL/transfer tables that are
visible, configurable, and still compatible with manual review when a course, training, or exam is
not yet listed; they also show that transfer and CPL credit can still vary by degree applicability,
residency, GPA thresholds, and other receiving-programme rules.

## B54

**Europass / European Commission (current 2025-2026). _European Digital Credentials for Learning_**
and **_Accreditation in the QDR_.**

URLs:
- https://europass.europa.eu/en/european-digital-credentials-learning
- https://europass.europa.eu/ro/stakeholders/qdr/accreditation

Why they matter: sharpen the archive's distinction between authenticity and equivalency by showing
how trusted digital credentials and accreditation checks can verify issuer status without settling
local recognition or credit decisions on their own.

## B55

**ANSI / Workcred (2026). _Strengthening Education and Workforce Training: Workcred and the National
Accreditation Commission Are Teaming Up_.**

URL: https://www.ansi.org/standards-news/all-news/3-18-26-strengthening-education-and-workforce-training-workcred

Why it matters: useful current signal that short-term workforce education is moving toward more
explicit criteria, structured evidence review, and scalable quality-assurance processes rather than
informal trust in provider claims.

## B56

**Credential Engine (current 2025-2026). _Simplifying Credential Data Management using Credential
Transparency Identifiers (CTIDs)_.**

URL: https://credentialengine.org/toolkit/simplifying-credential-data-management-using-credential-transparency-identifiers-ctids/

Why it matters: provides the archive's stable-identifier layer for machine-readable standing-list
publication, so public rules can reference specific offerings, organizations, and related resources
without ambiguous titles.

## B57

**1EdTech (current 2025-2026). _Open Badges_** and **_Comprehensive Learner Record (CLR Standard™)
and Open Badges Frequently Asked Questions_.**

URLs:
- https://www.1edtech.org/standards/open-badges
- https://www.1edtech.org/clr/faq

Why they matter: show that verifiable achievement records can publish structured metadata about
issuer, criteria, evidence, alignments, and expiration, which helps the archive define what a
minimal standing-list publication profile should point to.

## B58

**Europass / European Commission (current 2025-2026). _How to issue European Digital Credentials for
Learning_.**

URL: https://europass.europa.eu/en/how-issue-european-digital-credentials-learning

Why it matters: gives a public-sector template for achievement metadata such as title, description,
awarding body, linked learning outcomes, volume of learning, credits, learning setting, and
qualification context.

## B59

**Credential Engine / Digital Credentials Consortium (2025-2026). _Issuer Registries: Establishing
trust, privacy, and efficiency in verifying credential issuers_** and **_The Power of State
Credential Registries That Can Communicate_.**

URLs:
- https://credentialengine.org/resources/issuer-registries-establishing-trust-privacy-and-efficiency-in-verifying-credential-issuers/
- https://credentialengine.org/2026/01/28/the-power-of-state-credential-registries-that-can-communicate/

Why they matter: sharpen the archive's distinction between authenticity and equivalency while
reinforcing that open, interoperable publication matters if public recognition rules are meant to
travel across systems.



## B60

**1EdTech (current 2025-2026). _CASE v1.1 Specification_.**

URL: https://www.imsglobal.org/spec/case/v1p1

Why it matters: shows that outcome, rubric, and criterion references can be published as lightweight
structured associations, which helps the archive express construct-sensitive partial equivalency and
exception logic without falling back to title-matching alone.


## B61

**American Library Association (2025). _Privacy: An Interpretation of the Library Bill of Rights_.**

URL: https://www.ala.org/advocacy/intfreedom/librarybill/interpretations/privacy

Why it matters: gives the archive a concrete privacy-maintenance rule for public learning
infrastructure: analysis data should be anonymous or aggregated when possible, linked PII should be
minimized, retention should be reviewed, and analytics should not become behavior-tracking by
default.

## B62

**City University of New York (current 2025-2026). _Undergraduate Transfer Evaluation Services
(CCNY)_, _Transfer Credit Evaluation_ (Hostos), and _Academic Appeals Process_ (Baruch).**

URLs:
- https://www.ccny.cuny.edu/admissions/undergraduate-transfer-credit-evaluations
- https://www.hostos.cuny.edu/Administrative-Offices/Office-of-Admissions-and-Recruitment/College-Transfer-Credit-Policies
- https://weissman.baruch.cuny.edu/student-resources/academic-appeals-process/

Why they matter: provide a concrete public-system picture of reevaluation and appeal as
advisor/evaluator/department/faculty processes with local evidence review and, in some cases, final
central academic appeal—useful for designing a maintenance loop that learns from disputes without
pretending equivalency can be fully automated.

## B63

**U.S. Department of Labor (2025-2026). _Core Monitoring Guide_** and **_UIPL 14-25_.**

URLs:
- https://www.dol.gov/sites/dolgov/files/ETA/grants/PDFs/July%202025_CMG_508c-FINAL.pdf
- https://www.dol.gov/node/184215

Why they matter: supply the archive's corrective-action and continuous-improvement pattern:
performance data should be analyzed and acted on regularly, and inaccurate reporting should be
corrected rather than allowed to persist.

## B64

**U.S. Department of Education, Student Privacy Policy Office (current 2025-2026). _Legal Basics_**
and **_What is FERPA?_.**

URLs:
- https://studentprivacy.ed.gov/legal-basics
- https://studentprivacy.ed.gov/faq/what-ferpa

Why they matter: keep the archive honest that education-record systems must preserve rights of
inspection, correction/amendment, and control over disclosure of personally identifiable
information; those rights matter once recognition-maintenance data starts to look like an
educational record system rather than a harmless dashboard.

## B65

**Credential Engine (current 2025-2026). _Task Groups Archive_ (especially the CTDL Credential
Approval Lists Task Group and CTDL Transfer Value Profile Task Group).**

URL: https://credentialengine.org/taskgrouparchive/

Why it matters: shows that interoperable public lists can publish lifecycle status, list membership
periods, jurisdictional relevance, and transfer/recognition relationships rather than hiding those
rule states inside local documentation.

## B66

**W3C (2025). _Bitstring Status List v1.0_.**

URL: https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-bitstring-status-list/

Why it matters: provides the archive's privacy-sensitive status pattern: large grouped status lists
can publish revocation or suspension information while preserving group privacy better than
one-record-one-history publication.

## B67

**Europass / European Commission (current 2025-2026). _Using European Digital Credentials for
Learning_.**

URL: https://europass.europa.eu/en/stakeholders/european-digital-credentials/using-european-digital-credentials-learning

Why it matters: demonstrates a public verification posture built around clear status checks such as
validity, revocation, and accreditation rather than around publication of operational case logs.

## B68

**1EdTech (current 2025-2026). _Suggested Open Badges Requirements for Institutional RFP &
Procurement Agreements_.**

URL: https://www.1edtech.org/ob/rfp/resource

Why it matters: reinforces that revocation, expiration, evidence, and versioning are ordinary
publishable metadata concerns for learning credentials and should therefore shape the archive's
standing-entry history design.

## B69

**American Library Association (current 2025-2026). _Library Privacy Guidelines for Vendors_.**

URL: https://www.ala.org/advocacy/privacy/guidelines/vendors

Why it matters: gives the archive a concrete vendor-facing privacy boundary for public learning
infrastructure—minimize collection and retention, restrict sharing, preserve user access/correction,
and keep privacy obligations explicit in product and contract design.


## B70

**W3C (2019). _Verifiable Credentials Data Model 1.0_.**

URL: https://www.w3.org/TR/2019/REC-vc-data-model-20191119/

Why it matters: gives the archive its sharpest consumption rule: verifiers evaluate credential
status according to their own policy, issuers should distinguish cryptographic-integrity revocation
from ordinary status change, and refresh/replacement of a digital credential need not mean the
backing educational achievement is invalid.

## B71

**American Council on Education (current 2025-2026). _Colleges & Universities: Granting Credit With
the National Guide_.**

URL: https://www.acenet.edu/National-Guide/Pages/Granting-Credit.aspx

Why it matters: reinforces the archive's future-use bias by showing that ACE re-reviews evaluated
learning every three years to keep recommendations aligned with current expectations rather than
pretending one review settles all future versions forever.

## B72

**American Council on Education (current 2025-2026). _Learning Evaluations FAQ_.**

URL: https://www.acenet.edu/Programs-Services/Pages/Credit-Transcripts/CREDIT-Evaluations-FAQ.aspx

Why it matters: clarifies two points the archive needs for partner-consumption rules: actual credit
remains at the discretion of the receiving institution, and significant course/exam changes can
trigger technical review or audit instead of silent continued trust.

**City University of New York, College of Staten Island (current 2025-2026). _Registrar FAQ_.**

URL: https://www.csi.cuny.edu/students/registrar/frequently-asked-questions

Why it matters: supplies the archive's non-retroactivity analogy from ordinary academic governance:
degree requirements attach to a catalog year / declared-major baseline, and later policy change does
not automatically reopen every already-set learner pathway.

## B74

**Higher Learning Commission (2026). _HLC Policy Book_ (teach-out requirements).**

URL: https://download.hlcommission.org/policy/HLCPolicyBook_POL.pdf

Why it matters: gives the archive a current public rule for fair and equitable teach-out agreements,

records retention, and communications.

## B75

**Higher Learning Commission (2025). _Teach-Out Toolkit_.**

URL: https://download.hlcommission.org/initiatives/TeachOutToolkit_2020_RSC.pdf

transitional processes, advising, financial/logistical support, records release, and a best-practice
expectation of multiple transfer options plus teach-out planning by programme.

## B76

**SACSCOC (current 2025-2026). _Substantive Change Policy and Procedures_ (teach-out / closure
sections).**

URL: https://sacscoc.org/app/uploads/2019/08/SubstantiveChange.pdf

- https://www.ed.gov/media/document/clsdschldefpdf-58803.pdf

**CAST (current 2025-2026). _UDL Guidelines 3.0_.**

once the contracted function ends.

## B130

**Digital Promise (2026). _Evaluating AI in Education: An Analysis of State Guidance_.**

URL: https://digitalpromise.org/2026/01/26/evaluating-ai-in-education-an-analysis-of-state-guidance/

Why it matters: gives the archive a compact maturity signal by distinguishing exploratory, piloting,
and systematic-evidentiary stages, while explicitly arguing that broader adoption should wait for
clearer evidence of impact on learning.

## B131

**SETDA (2025). _2025 State EdTech Trends Report_.**

URL: https://www.setda.org/priorities/state-trends/

Why it matters: adds an operational warning that AI may become a top policy priority faster than
states build durable funding or professional-learning capacity, which matters directly for deciding
whether a successful pilot is actually ready for scale.

## B132

**Institute of Education Sciences / REL Central (2026). _Big Questions, Early Signals: AI and School
Turnaround_.**

URL: https://ies.ed.gov/learn/blog/big-questions-early-signals-ai-and-school-turnaround

Why it matters: sharpens the archive's post-pilot judgment by showing a mixed evidence picture:
stronger support for some student-facing tutoring uses, emerging but less settled evidence for
teacher-support tools, and ambiguous returns from some predictive or early-warning applications.

## B133

**Stanford SCALE / AI Hub for Education (2026). _The Evidence Base on AI in K-12: A 2026 Review_.**

URL: https://scale.stanford.edu/research-in-action/understanding-evidence-base-ai-k12-education

Why it matters: synthesizes the rapidly growing K-12 AI literature while showing that rigorous
causal evidence is still thin and concentrated in a relatively small set of use cases, which is
directly useful for deciding when a promising pilot should scale, stay local, or remain under a
governed ceiling.

## B134

**Institute of Education Sciences / NCER (2024). _Transformative Research in the Education Sciences
(FY 2025 competition notice)_.**

URL: https://ies.ed.gov/ncer/2025/01/transformative-research-education-sciences-fy-2025-84-305t

Why it matters: although it is a research-funding notice rather than an education-AI policy memo, it
gives the archive a useful official scaling signal by naming implementation contrast, cost analysis,
generalization, and support for scaling as distinct requirements rather than treating promising
early effects as enough on their own.

## B135

**U.S. Department of Labor / Employment and Training Administration (2026). _Training and Employment
Notice No. 07-25: The U.S. Department of Labor’s Artificial Intelligence Literacy Framework_.**

URL: https://www.dol.gov/agencies/eta/advisories/ten-07-25

Why it matters: gives the archive a current public-route signal that workforce boards, American Job
Centers, community colleges, apprenticeship agencies, and state education systems should treat AI
literacy and navigation support as shared public infrastructure, which helps distinguish
informational route help from hotter queue-shaping or allocation systems.

## B136

**UK Department for Education (updated 2025-2026). _Generative artificial intelligence (AI) in
education_.**

URL: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/generative-artificial-intelligence-in-education/generative-artificial-intelligence-ai-in-education

Why it matters: gives the archive a current deployment signal that teacher-facing AI use has more
immediate benefits and fewer risks than pupil-facing use, while pupil-facing use needs stronger
safeguards, careful data protection, and visible handling of automated decision-making and
profiling.

## B137

**OECD (2026). _Designing safe AI systems for education_.**

URL: https://www.oecd.org/en/blogs/2026/01/designing-safe-ai-systems-for-education.html

Why it matters: adds a current OECD safety signal that age-appropriate design, explicit human
authority, and rejection of emotional manipulation or pseudo-friend roles are central when
generative AI is used with learners.

## B138

**UNESCO (2025). _AI and education: Protecting the rights of learners_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/ai-and-education-protecting-rights-learners

Why it matters: reinforces a human-centred, rights-based floor for educational AI by tying
personalization and management claims to privacy, safety, equity, and accountability rather than
product convenience alone.

## B139

**UK Information Commissioner's Office (current 2025-2026). _What if we want to profile children or
make automated decisions about them?_**

URL: https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/childrens-information/children-and-the-uk-gdpr/what-if-we-want-to-profile-children-or-make-automated-decisions-about-them/

Why it matters: adds a current child-data protection signal that profiling children or making
automated decisions about them is presumptively high-risk, requires a DPIA, and should not proceed
without human intervention, contestability, intelligible notice, and strong justification.


## B140

**UK Information Commissioner's Office (current 2025-2026). _Legal framework_ in _Explaining
decisions made with AI_.**

URL: https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/artificial-intelligence/explaining-decisions-made-with-artificial-intelligence/part-1-the-basics-of-explaining-ai/legal-framework/

Why it matters: makes the archive's `M3` rail schema more concrete by tying AI-assisted decisions to
meaningful information about logic and consequences, the rights to human intervention / expression
of view / contestation where significant automated treatment exists, and a living-DPIA expectation
for high-risk profiling or automated evaluation.

## B141

**U.S. Department of Education, Protecting Student Privacy (current official FERPA text). _FERPA_
and _Legal Basics_.**

URL: https://studentprivacy.ed.gov/ferpa and https://studentprivacy.ed.gov/legal-basics

Why it matters: sharpens the archive's `M3` record-rail floor by making inspect/review rights,
amendment and hearing rights, statement-of-disagreement preservation, consent-bound disclosure, and
non-destruction during active review visible in a current official source rather than only in older
vendor-guidance summaries.

## B142

**UK Department for Education (2026). _Working together to safeguard children_.**

URL: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/working-together-to-safeguard-children--2

Why it matters: gives the archive its clearest current statutory signal that child-serving urgent
routing is a distinct multi-agency regime rather than an ordinary queue exception: education
providers sit inside shared safeguarding duties with social care, police, health, and even
housing/homelessness services, which supports naming a narrow `QS-UD-CHILD-SAFETY` branch while
keeping most other urgent-duty cases local.

## B143

**UK Information Commissioner's Office (current 2025-2026). _Rights related to automated decision
making including profiling_.**

URL: https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/individual-rights/individual-rights/rights-related-to-automated-decision-making-including-profiling/

Why it matters: gives the archive a current operational rights floor that deployers should provide
information about relevant processing, simple ways to request human intervention or challenge a
decision, and regular checks that systems affecting people still work as intended.

URL: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/ai-playbook-for-the-uk-government/artificial-intelligence-playbook-for-the-uk-government-html

Why it matters: adds a current operational transparency signal that public bodies should document

the public.

## B145

URL: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/data-ethics-framework/data-and-ai-ethics-framework

## B146

and post-market monitoring plan for high-risk AI systems_.**

URL: https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/article-72

Why it matters: gives the archive a current official lifecycle-governance signal that high-risk AI
providers should actively and systematically collect and analyse post-market data throughout the
system's lifetime so that compliance and emerging risks are checked after deployment rather than
assumed.

## B147

**European Commission / AI Act Service Desk (2026). _Article 9: Risk management system_.**

URL: https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/article-9

Why it matters: adds a current official signal that risk management for high-risk AI should be a
continuous iterative process with regular systematic review and updating, which is directly useful
for deciding when a cooled or office-bound default has actually earned recovery rather than merely
enjoyed a quiet interval.

## B148

**European Commission / AI Act Service Desk (2026). _Article 73: Reporting of serious incidents_.**

URL: https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/article-73

Why it matters: adds a current official lifecycle-governance signal that serious incidents on
high-risk AI systems should trigger formal reporting and investigation rather than being treated as
ordinary local variance, which directly supports the archive's narrower relapse rule for re-hardened
authority-family defaults.

## B149

**European Commission / AI Act Service Desk (2026). _Recital 128_.**

URL: https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/recital-128

Why it matters: gives the archive a current official reset signal that changes affecting compliance,
or changes to intended purpose, should be treated as a new AI system requiring a new conformity
assessment rather than inheriting old trust by default.

## B150

**European Commission (2026). _Navigating the AI Act_.**

URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/faqs/navigating-ai-act

Why it matters: sharpens the archive's post-recovery watch rule by tying intended purpose to risk
classification and by making deployer-side monitoring, human oversight, action on identified risks
or serious incidents, and explanation duties visible in one current official implementation source.

outcomes_.**

URL: https://openai.com/index/understanding-ai-and-learning-outcomes/

Why it matters: gives the archive a current measurement signal that institutions should assess
longitudinal learning outcomes rather than infer educational value from task performance,
satisfaction, or short-run usage alone.

## B152

**UNESCO (2026). _AI competency framework for teachers_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/ai-competency-framework-teachers

## B153

**UNESCO (2026). _AI competency framework for students_.**

default_.**

**Higher Learning Commission (2026). _HLC Policy Book_.** and **Office for Students (2025).

Why they matter: sharpen the archive's reserve-depletion rule by making two limits explicit at once
— receiving institutions must have real capacity for teach-out work, and serious closure/continuity

Arrangements (FDCR.B.10.010)_** and **_Teach-Out Toolkit_.**

- https://download.hlcommission.org/initiatives/TeachOutToolkit_2020_RSC.pdf

learner-protection facts explicit at once — receiving institutions must have real capacity and
support services, teach-out communications should visibly publish timelines / contacts / record and
aid help, and best-practice teach-out planning should prioritize students nearest completion while
offering more than one plausible downstream destination where possible.

student-protection mitigations must be fair and reasonable in light of mobility, parity of course

Why they matter: sharpen the archive's switch-rights taper rule by making six route-consumption
facts explicit at once — transfer arrangements should already be published; contingency planning
should include mapped alternatives, record-transfer readiness, and student support; supportive
services are participation-enabling, locally limited, and exception-capable; estimated aid or
memo-entry handling is not yet a disbursement; actual disbursement creates real rights and
responsibilities; and withdrawal / post-withdrawal handling creates real timing and return
obligations that make some financial states legally sticky without turning every pre-start
administrative step into irreversible route commitment.

institutional-closure planning should include alternative-study mapping, student support, complaint

reasonable opportunity to complete; and actual Title IV disbursement creates real legal and
financial states without making every pre-disbursement administrative motion irreversible.

circumstances change; student-protection work should stay current, implemented, and closure-ready;
providers should plan mapped alternatives, record transfer, communications, and support rather than
leaving restarted students to rediscover routes alone; workforce supportive services remain
participation-enabling and locally administered; memo-entry or estimated aid is not yet a real
disbursement; real disbursement and withdrawal treatment create real legal and financial states that
cannot be hand-waved away; and teach-out failure that leaves no reasonable opportunity to complete
can justify escalation beyond mere rerouting or offset treatment.

debt-relief or discharge guidance that must not be hidden behind an institutional credit, waiver, or
reroute offer.

eCFR). _34 CFR 685.214 — Closed school discharge_.** and **U.S. Department of Education, Federal
Student Aid (2025-2026). _Disbursing Title IV Funds_.**

Why they matter: sharpen the archive's escalation-packet rule by making seven things visible at once
— teach-out communications must tell affected students the applicable period, scope, accepted-credit
treatment, tuition / fees, and records plan; student-protection materials must be public, current,
and clear about continuation, communication, refunds / compensation, support, and complaints;
provider-closure expectations now explicitly include support to understand options, access to
evidence of academic achievement, refunds / compensation, and complaint handling; workforce
supportive-service regimes still run through local policies with limits and exceptions; federal
closure rules require explanation of discharge procedures and eligibility, disclosure of third-party
recovery, and production of supporting documentation; rights-transfer on discharge is limited to the
amount discharged and does not wipe out unrelated relief; and estimated aid is not the same thing as
actual disbursement, so a notice packet must distinguish pending ledger predictions from legally
real charge or loan states.

students from closed-school-discharge information aimed only at potentially eligible students; OfS
requires student protection plans to be clear, accessible, current, regularly revised, and explicit
about triggers; OfS's student-facing closure guidance expects information and support relevant to
each student's specific circumstances, including options, transferability, recognition,
refunds/compensation, support contacts, and complaints; its provider-closure directions explicitly
include option-mapping, evidence-of-achievement access, support to understand options,
refunds/compensation, and complaint handling; workforce supportive-service regimes remain locally
conditioned by necessity, limits, and exceptions rather than one cohort-wide entitlement statement;
actual Title IV disbursement and credit-balance duties are legally different from memo entries and
estimates; and closed-school / failed-teach-out rights can become newly live, individually, as
teach-out completion fails, time passes, or third-party recovery and documentation facts change.

treatment, tuition / fees, records treatment, and applicable period; OfS still requires
student-protection planning to preserve continuation, offer fair and reasonable mitigations, and
publish clear, current, accessible plans, and its student-facing closure guidance still expects
information and support relevant to specific circumstances, including timescales, alternative study
options, transferability, recognition, refunds / compensation, contacts, and complaints; OIA still
runs an external complaint clock from the Completion of Procedures Letter rather than from vague
earlier confusion; FAFSA Submission Summary figures remain estimates rather than school offers; the
school’s aid offer remains the best source of truth for exact aid; and Title IV authorizations still
must be informed, conspicuous, cancellable, and nonretroactive. Together those signals support a
tight archive rule: provisional packets may support navigation and reversible holds, but materially
ambiguous route / money / rights fields should not drive binding learner elections or silently
running adverse clocks.

Why they matter: sharpen the archive's restart-fairness rule by making six things visible at once —
HLC still requires teach-out communications to state scope, applicable period, and other
student-critical terms and still allows new provisional plans when circumstances change; OfS still
requires student-protection measures to be fair and reasonable for students, to reflect student
diversity and needs, and to stay current, while its 2025 closure brief says communications should
foreground students’ likely concerns, explain impact and options fully, and account for reasonable
adjustments; OIA still expects written outcomes to explain what action the student needs to take and
any deadlines, still says completion letters should issue promptly, and still runs its external
clock from the Completion of Procedures Letter; and Federal Student Aid still requires
authorizations to explain the covered period, how to cancel or modify, and that cancellation is
nonretroactive. Together those signals support a tight archive rule: once a paused clock can
truthfully restart, the learner is owed a fresh lane-specific notice, a non-surprise grace floor for
adverse deadlines, and reminder treatment that cannot be used to smuggle in a second substantive
change.

plan, rather than assuming one undifferentiated student clock; OfS still requires mitigations to be
fair and reasonable for students with different needs and to stay current, while its student-facing

letter to state the final decision, reasons, and deadline; Title IV still draws a hard line between
estimates / memo entries and actual disbursement, with disbursement date creating real recipient
rights and responsibilities; and current closed-school discharge rules still treat failed teach-out
performance as a separate rights trigger. Together those signals support a tight archive rule:
restarted route, money, and external-rights clocks may share one date only when they truly restart
from the same clarified event.

testing guidance shows those response-recording accommodations remain live in current testing
operations; the AP accommodations guide still expects a reviewable student response rather than
secret surrogate authorship; AP AI guidance keeps inquiry, coding assistance, and source-finding
distinct from final student-owned performance and explanation; and WIDA keeps the validity line
explicit by prohibiting modifications that change what the assessment measures. Together those
signals support a tighter archive rule: later supported reading access and language-project inquiry
can harden sooner, ordinary writing can harden only with a recording-not-rewriting protected-access
carveout, and direct-production rows like first-pass decoding and no-executor symbolic procedure
still need hotter branching before stronger inheritance.

supports/accommodations should be used, and modifications can invalidate results; College Board's

survive any pause, substitute, or restart decision.

dismissal order and answer-status verification visible, so unresolved digital submission now
truthfully supports a status / device / deadline / next-owner handback shell rather than a generic
reassurance; current hybrid digital AP pages say the authoritative free-response artifact is a paper
booklet whose cover carries student identification and an AP ID label, while AP return guidance says
secure exam materials must be returned to the AP Program, which together support a
collected-into-custody handback shell rather than a learner self-submit fiction; and current AP
Digital Portfolio, AP Capstone policy, AP accommodations, and AP Research materials keep written
submission deadlines, teacher checkpoint/authenticity affirmations, no-preview assistant boundaries,
and one-academic-year local defense-record retention distinct. Together those signals support a
tighter archive rule: the next portable publication move after artifact-attached timing shells is
not a universal incident form but artifact-specific receipt / handback / owner-notice minima.

no universal self-service menu, concrete request family, request object, strongest official effect

  rather than durable all-copy repeat post-third-dewatch monitoring).
- Google Search Help, "Find and remove personal info in Google Search results", "Remove my private
  info from Google Search", and related personal-content surfaces (current help pages; Search
  removal / source-page separation, request statuses, notifications, screenshot and contact-info
  proof, new-result handling, and sensitive proof boundaries).
- Princeton Site Builder Documentation, "Deleting Content", "Content Management", and "Cache"
  (current university documentation; local deletion, document/media-library cleanup, access control,
  cache behavior, Google/Bing search-result behavior, custom-search exclusion, Wayback persistence,
  and public-web persistence caveat).
- WiscWeb, "Emergency Site Edit Policy", "Options for protecting pages", "Edits Not Showing Up", and
  related WiscWeb guidance (current university documentation; local WiscWeb source edits,
  primary-contact follow-up, redirection/launch prerequisites, campus-search and lookahead owner
  separation, caching, normal-hours support limits, and no WiscWeb control over major-search-engine
  status/timing/expedition).
- Rowan University Division of Marketing & Communication, current Web Services,
  digital-accessibility, Cascade CMS, Siteimprove, contributor-training, Web Ambassador,
  content-audit/redesign, project closeout, and support surfaces (current university documentation;
  Cascade CMS ownership, Siteimprove/accessibility monitoring, content-audit/redesign review,
  contributor training, support tickets, QA, project/service requests, and
  accessibility/accommodation routing).
- Internet Archive Help Center, "How do I request to remove something from archive.org?" and "Using
  the Wayback Machine" (current help pages; URL, time-period, control-period, review-context
  prerequisites, site-owner exclusion routes, capture/time-slice specificity, and no automatic
  all-copy outcome).
- Bing Webmaster Tools official help surfaces, including content removal / URL blocking /
  outdated-content and provider-facing documentation (current help pages and provider-facing
  documentation; temporary blocking, up-to-90-day expiry, cached/outdated-content handling,
  extension or permanent-removal prerequisites, exact URL submission, and source-side permanence
  methods rather than one cross-engine post-third-dewatch threshold certificate).

Why they matter: support the archive's first repeat post-third-dewatch relapse / third-repair
rewatch-threshold defaults for hot-exam recipient followup shells by keeping nineteen distinctions
visible at once — current official pages expose exact URL/image requirements, current/expired status
histories, temporary and route-native expiry, source-side permanent methods, sensitive proof limits,
local source control, cache/search/archive separation, protected-route and accessibility ownership,
recipient or official-record dependencies, archive time/control-period proof, provider-control
limits, owner-specific support routes, redesign/project boundaries, third-exit proof minimization,
later recurrence limits, post-third-dewatch memory boundaries, source/search/archive split limits,
route-native proof control, and repeat-threshold ownership; they do not expose one automatic
fourth-rewatch trigger, public serial-repair stigma label, duplicate learner-filing duty, or public
third-rewatch proof vault. Together those signals support a tighter archive rule: hot recipient
followup shells may now carry one tiny `ZF0-ZF4` layer for no automatic fourth rewatch / permanent
serial stigma / duplicate filing / public third-repair vault / repeat-by-anecdote, repeat
post-third-dewatch count and third-repair scope publication, rewatch-threshold source / owner /
proof-age / memory-limit publication, learner-safe action separated from threshold owner work, and
threshold disposition / minimization / reopen triggers, but they still do not justify one permanent
public serial-repair dashboard, one duplicate filing rule, one all-surface third-repair certificate,
one protected-proof publication duty, or one no-memory reset.

## B275

**NIST (2024). _Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework: Generative Artificial
Intelligence Profile (NIST AI 600-1)_.**

URL: https://www.nist.gov/publications/artificial-intelligence-risk-management-framework-generative-artificial-intelligence

Why it matters: gives a cross-sector risk-management frame for generative AI systems, including
risks around human-AI configuration, data privacy, information integrity, information security, and
value-chain / component integration; useful for turning education procurement into a system-risk
register rather than a feature checklist.

## B276

**European Commission (2025-2026). _AI literacy — Questions & Answers_ and Article 4 implementation
guidance for the EU AI Act.**

URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/faqs/ai-literacy-questions-answers

Why it matters: confirms that AI-literacy duties are now an operational governance obligation for
deployers and providers, not just a curricular aspiration; useful for linking staff training, user
context, affected persons, and system-risk awareness.

## B277

**EDSAFE AI Alliance (2026). _S.A.F.E. by Design: Policy, Research, and Practice Recommendations for
AI Companions in Education_.**

URL: https://www.edsafeai.org/safieaichatbots

Why it matters: moves the archive's companion concern beyond generic chatbot ethics into
student-safety, youth-rights, companion-dependency, and platform-agnostic safeguarding design.

## B278

**Assessment-authority AI policy signals: College Board AP AI tool guidance, Cambridge International
generative-AI coursework policy, International Baccalaureate AI in learning / teaching / assessment
guidance, and UNESCO assessment-in-the-AI-age work.**

URLs:
- https://apcentral.collegeboard.org/exam-administration-ordering-scores/administering-exams/exam-policies/artificial-intelligence-tools
- https://www.cambridgeinternational.org/exam-administration/cambridge-exams-officers-guide/phase-3-coursework-and-moderation/generative-ai-in-coursework
- https://ibo.org/programmes/artificial-intelligence-ai-in-learning-teaching-and-assessment/
- https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/whats-worth-measuring-future-assessment-ai-age

Why they matter: support a construct-first assessment rule: AI may be appropriate for inquiry,
support, and quality control in some contexts, but the assessed work must remain the learner's where
that is the construct, human examiners or accountable humans remain central to high-stakes marking,
and assessment should shift toward higher-order thinking, ethical reasoning, authentic proof, and
clear AI-use boundaries.

## B279

**Mixed 2025-2026 learning-evidence signals: Tutor CoPilot RCT, Kestin et al. AI-tutor RCT in
_Scientific Reports_, Barcaui et al. unrestricted ChatGPT / retention RCT, and RAND 2026 student AI
homework survey.**

URLs:
- https://scale.stanford.edu/publications/tutor-copilot-human-ai-approach-scaling-real-time-expertise
- https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-025-97652-6
- https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2590291125010186
- https://www.rand.org/pubs/research_reports/RRA4742-1.html

Why they matter: keep the archive from becoming either AI boosterism or AI prohibition.
Pedagogically shaped tutor support can improve measured learning in studied settings, while
unrestricted answer support can reduce durable retention and students themselves report
critical-thinking concern. The archive should therefore publish cognitive-effort budgets rather than
treating all AI help as equivalent.

## B280

**OWASP Gen AI Security Project (2025). _OWASP Top 10 for LLMs and Gen AI Apps_.**

URL: https://genai.owasp.org/llm-top-10/

Why it matters: supplies a practical adversarial-risk vocabulary for prompt injection, insecure
output handling, data poisoning, denial-of-service, supply-chain risk, sensitive information
disclosure, excessive agency, and related LLM application failures; useful for treating education AI
integrations as workflows that can be attacked, not just chat interfaces that can be moderated.

## B281

**Ofqual (2026). _Principles of AI use in marking_.**

URL: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/principles-of-ai-use-in-marking/principles-of-ai-use-in-marking

Why it matters: keeps high-stakes marking and qualification decisions anchored in validity,
fairness, transparency, and accountable human responsibility; useful for capping action authority
where AI touches grades, qualifications, or official educational records.

## B282

**Education Endowment Foundation (2024-2026). _ChatGPT in lesson preparation — Teacher Choices
trial_ and related EdTech evaluation agenda.**

URLs:
- https://educationendowmentfoundation.org.uk/projects-and-evaluation/projects/choices-in-edtech-using-generative-ai-chatgpt-for-ks3-science-lesson-preparation-2024-teacher-choices-trial
- https://educationendowmentfoundation.org.uk/projects-and-evaluation/research-agenda-themes-priority-areas/research-agenda-theme-edtech

Why it matters: gives the archive a concrete independent-evaluation signal for teacher workload
claims, including the need to distinguish time-saving claims from learning, validity, access,
security, and downstream review-burden claims.

## B283

**U.S. Department of Education (2025). _Artificial Intelligence use in schools and federal education
programs — Dear Colleague / AI guidance surfaces_.**

URLs:
- https://www.ed.gov/about/news/press-release/us-department-of-education-issues-guidance-artificial-intelligence-use-schools-proposes-additional-supplemental-priority
- https://www.ed.gov/about/ed-overview/artificial-intelligence-ai-guidance

Why it matters: supports the archive's deployment posture that AI can be used for educational
functions only inside existing statutory, regulatory, privacy, accessibility, and program
requirements; useful for sector adapters, public-summary limits, and implementation stop rules.

## B284

**Digital Promise (2026). _Evaluating AI in Education: An Analysis of State Guidance_ and report
_What States Say About Evaluating AI in Education: Reviewing Guidance from 32 States and Puerto Rico_.**

URLs:
- https://digitalpromise.org/2026/01/26/evaluating-ai-in-education-an-analysis-of-state-guidance/
- https://digitalpromise.dspacedirect.org/server/api/core/bitstreams/5e4b912f-9e2e-4814-879d-289946d1ce54/content

Why it matters: supports the rev0230 sprint posture. Many state guidance efforts remain exploratory
or piloting rather than systematic and evidentiary, so the next useful move is a local feedback loop
with qualitative and quantitative metrics, not a broad scale claim.

## B285

**U.S. Department of Education / Federal Register (2026). _Final Priority and Definitions:
Secretary's Supplemental Priority and Definitions on Advancing Artificial Intelligence in
Education_.**

URL: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/04/13/2026-07087/final-priority-and-definitions-secretarys-supplemental-priority-and-definitions-on-advancing

Why it matters: adds a current federal policy signal that AI education projects remain inside
existing federal education, disability, civil-rights, accessibility, and documentation obligations,
and that universal design / accessibility is not optional implementation decoration.

## B286

**Federal Trade Commission (2025-2026). _Children's Online Privacy Protection Rule updates and COPPA
business guidance_.**

URLs:
- https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2025/01/ftc-finalizes-changes-childrens-privacy-rule-limiting-companies-ability-monetize-kids-data
- https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/privacy-security/childrens-privacy
- https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2026/02/ftc-issues-coppa-policy-statement-incentivize-use-age-verification-technologies-protect-children

Why it matters: reinforces the first-import aggregate-only stance for minor-facing tools. The
archive should not ask for raw learner traces, unnecessary child personal information, or retained
records when decision-grade aggregates and owner attestations can answer the import question.

## B287

**European Union (2024). _Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 — Artificial Intelligence Act_.**

URL: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/PDF/?uri=OJ:L_202401689

Why it matters: identifies education and vocational training uses that are high-risk, including AI
for access or admission, assignment to institutions or programmes, evaluation of learning outcomes,
assessment of education level, and monitoring prohibited behaviour during tests; useful for keeping
`FT-0181` inside bounded course operations and out of high-risk assessment/admission lanes.

## B288

**UNESCO (2023). _Guidance for generative AI in education and research_.**

URL: https://www.unesco.org/en/articles/guidance-generative-ai-education-and-research

Why it matters: reinforces data privacy, age-appropriate human-agent design, and pedagogical
validation as explicit deployment constraints; useful for youth-safeguarding defaults and the
education AI deployment risk crosswalk.

## B289

**New York City Public Schools (2026). _Guidance on Artificial Intelligence_.**

URL: https://www.schools.nyc.gov/about-us/vision-and-mission/artificial-intelligence/guidance-on-artificial-intelligence

Why it matters: supplies a concrete district implementation model for AI tool review, student-data
boundaries, human review, and limits on consequential outputs; useful for translating abstract
privacy and oversight rules into school-operational defaults.

## B290

**Consortium for School Networking (2025-2026). _Student data privacy resources and State of EdTech
leadership signals_.**

URLs:
- https://www.cosn.org/edtech-topics/student-data-privacy/
- https://www.cosn.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/05/EdTechLeadership_2025_F2.pdf

Why it matters: keeps implementation burden, cybersecurity, student data privacy, and district
capacity visible; useful for preventing the archive from treating privacy compliance as a solved
paper exercise when schools still face practical safeguards and resourcing constraints.

## B291

**European Commission (2026). _Guidelines for providers and deployers of high-risk AI systems under the AI Act_.**

URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/guidelines-ai-high-risk-systems

Why it matters: adds current classification guidance and practical examples for high-risk AI
systems under the AI Act. The archive should use it as a refresh trigger for education use cases
near admission, programme assignment, learning-outcome evaluation, level-of-education decisions,
or test-behaviour monitoring, and should keep `FT-0181` confined to bounded course operations.
